TEXT A Part 0 "What this document claims":
"An explanation is a **question-relevant organization of dependencies**, held to a target by a **transport** that is checked only by **what changes when things are changed**. Nothing in the definition of an explanation asks whether a piece of the explanation "is the same kind of thing" as a piece of the world. That question has no independent content: at any level of detail, a *kind* is nothing over and above how a component responds to the changes that level admits. Two components no admitted change can separate are one kind at that level, whatever labels anyone attaches to them."

"Correspondence between a representation and what it represents is not a primitive of this semantics. It is a **relation with a provenance**. A correspondence can be *selected* — produced by blind variation and survival on a history of predictions — or *constructed* — produced by an episode of conjecture and criticism — or merely *declared* by whoever writes the model down. The three are distinguished by their histories, not their outputs, and the semantics keeps them apart. Creativity lives in construction. Selection produces the raw material construction works on. Declaration is permitted only as a modelling convenience, and a claim about creativity cannot rest on it."

TEXT B's version: "Correspondence between a representation and what it represents is not a primitive here. It is a **relation with a provenance**. A correspondence can be *selected*, produced by variation and survival on a history of encountered changes; or *constructed*, produced by an episode of conjecture and criticism; or merely *declared* by whoever writes the model down. The three are told apart by their histories, not their outputs, and the semantics keeps them apart (Part IV). ..."

OK here's my refined principle, trying to match the apparent design: a difference is CLAIM if one text asserts a proposition (verdict, requirement, classification) that the other text does not state and that is not trivially a restatement; WORDING if the texts state the same proposition in different words/order/detail, or if the added/dropped material is a cross-reference, or a restatement of something the other text states at some place.

Under this:
- D18 (measure): B states a verdict A doesn't state (even though A's principles imply it) → CLAIM (both lists agree).
- D29 (packaging): B states a verdict A doesn't → CLAIM (both agree).
- D16 (finer contract): A states it in Grievance 2 ("At a finer level with more admitted changes they may separate") → WORDING (both agree).
- D34 (theorem applicability): A doesn't state "the theorem applies only where its assumptions hold" — it states the theorem with its hypotheses and gives the interference case. Is "the theorem applies only where its assumptions hold" a restatement of "If Γ is finite, S is upward closed, and Γ∈S, then..."? Yes — a conditional's content is that it applies where its antecedent holds. And "an addition to Γ that destroys a support is the interference case below, and there upward closure fails" — this labels the interference example as a case of failed upward closure, which A's example displays (S = {{a}} with Γ = {a,b} is not upward closed since {a} ∈ S but {a,b} ⊇... wait upward closure: if W ∈ S and W ⊆ W' then W' ∈ S. S = {{a}}: {a} ∈ S but {a,b} ∉ S → not upward closed ✓ visible in A). So B's addition is a restatement of what A's text displays → WORDING. OK, agreeing with AUDIT D34. Good.

So principle: CLAIM when the added/dropped proposition is not stated or plainly displayed in the other text; WORDING when it is stated elsewhere or is the plain content of what's displayed (including conditional statements' scope), or is a pointer.

AUDIT D33: B: "Criticality is relative to the support \(W\) it is assessed in: a commitment critical in one successful support need not be critical in the full candidate, and the supports assessed are the ones actually written, not a support someone could write in their place." ✓ MATCH.

AUDIT D45: B: "The subhistory in Build is owned by \(s\) when its processes run inside the system boundary and resource contract declared for \(s\) (Part XII). AUDIT D55: B: "A capability is attributed to a system under a declared boundary (which processes and resources are the system's) and a declared continuity \(\Omega\) (what makes it the same system through change). A replaced part that preserves the declared continuity leaves the same system; a process run inside the boundary is the system's whoever wrote it; a process run outside it is not the system's however close it sits. Both are declared before the attribution, not chosen after it." ✓ Text B Part XII. MATCH.

AUDIT D56: B: "Ownership is grounded in the processes and resources the boundary includes, never in the capability being attributed: "owned because it can, and can because owned" grounds neither." ✓ MATCH.

AUDIT D59: B: "Besides the two primitives, some claims take stated inputs that the semantics records and does not supply: the obligations \(O\) and \(P\) of a repair, with the occasions each covers (Part XI); the scope of a contract and what makes a restriction appropriate (Part III); the system boundary and continuity of an attribution (Part XII). A verdict that depends on one of these is a verdict given the input; where the input is missing, the verdict is unsettled and the semantics says so rather than choosing the input from the verdict wanted." ✓ MATCH.

AUDIT D65: A: "Let \(t\) be selected on a finite history \(H\subsetneq C\). For every \((a,b)\in C\setminus H\) there exists a transport \(t'\), also surviving on \(H\), with a different value at \((a,b)\)." ✓ Text A Der 3. B: "Let \(t\) be selected from a population \(\mathcal T\) on a finite history \(H\subsetneq C\). For every \((a,b)\in C\setminus H\) at which some \(t'\in\mathcal T\), also surviving on \(H\), has a different value from \(t\), the value of \(t\) at \((a,b)\) is underdetermined by \(H\): survival on \(H\) does not distinguish \(t\) from \(t'\) there. The presence of an unseen pair alone does not establish that such a \(t'\) exists; it must be admitted, realizable, a member of \(\mathcal T\), and a survivor of \(H\)." ✓ MATCH.

AUDIT D66: A: "Alter \(L_{j}(a,b)\) for one \((a,b)\notin H\) to any other admitted relation; the result survives on \(H\) and differs at \((a,b)\). ∎" ✓ Text A. B: "Where \(\mathcal T\) contains a transport with \(L_{j}(a,b)\) altered to another admitted relation for one \((a,b)\notin H\), that transport survives on \(H\) and differs at \((a,b)\), and survival on \(H\) cannot select between the two. Where \(\mathcal T\) contains no such transport, \(H\) is silent on the value at \((a,b)\) and the population fixes it. ∎" ✓ MATCH.

AUDIT D67: A: "A correspondence produced by selection is faithful where it was tested and unconstrained where it was not." ✓. B: "A correspondence produced by selection is faithful where it was tested and, wherever its population admits an alternative, unconstrained where it was not. This is why the primitive layer is fallible: it was shaped against the changes its history contained. It is also why surprise is possible. What the qualification gives up is the guarantee of a differing survivor at every unseen pair, and with it the blanket claim that every untested value is unconstrained; a population restriction, a physical relation, or another stated constraint may already fix a value that the history never tested." ✓ MATCH. ### NEW1
PART: Part 0 — Where to attack this (B) / Part XV — (B) Necessity
A: "Produce a genuine explanation whose organization cannot be captured by *any* transport satisfying component fidelity under *any* physically admitted contract."
B: "A genuine explanation whose organization no transport can preserve under any physically admitted contract."
WHY: A demands a counterexample that no component-faithful (F1) transport can capture, B one that no faithful (F1 and F2) transport can preserve, so B's refutation condition is weaker than A's front-matter one even though A's Part XV uses B's wording.
TEST: A genuine explanation captured by some (F1)-only transport but preserved by no transport satisfying (F2) as well: A's attack (B) does not count it as refuting necessity, B's does.
